Diffusion Model for Manifold Data: Score Decomposition, Curvature, and Statistical Complexity
Zixuan Zhang, Kaixuan Huang, Tuo Zhao, Mengdi Wang, Minshuo Chen

Abstract
Diffusion models have become a leading framework in generative modeling, yet their theoretical understanding—especially for high-dimensional data concentrated on low-dimensional structures—remains incomplete. This paper investigates how diffusion models learn such structured data, focusing on two key aspects: statistical complexity and influence of data geometric properties. By modeling data as samples from a smooth Riemannian manifold, our analysis reveals crucial decompositions of score functions in diffusion models under different levels of injected noise. We also highlight the interplay of manifold curvature with the structures in the score function. These analyses enable an efficient neural network approximation to the score function, built upon which we further provide statistical rates for score estimation and distribution learning. Remarkably, the obtained statistical rates are governed by the intrinsic dimension of data and the manifold curvature. These results advance the statistical foundations of diffusion models, bridging theory and practice for generative modeling on manifolds.



1
Introduction

Diffusion models have recently emerged as a powerful framework in generative modeling, surpassing the performance of traditional methods like Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs,
26
) and Variational AutoEncoders (VAEs,
36
) in many applications
(
30
;
59
;
58
;
54
)
. Diffusion models have found success across a variety of domains, including image synthesis
(
19
;
31
;
59
)
, audio generation
(
37
;
73
)
, and natural language processing
(
4
;
45
;
49
)
, and have even extended their influence into areas like computational biology
(
71
;
27
)
, reinforcement learning and control
(
74
;
17
;
13
)
. Despite their empirical success, a comprehensive theoretical understanding of diffusion models—especially in high-dimensional settings—remains limited, and further exploration is essential to advance both their theoretical and practical potential.

At their core, diffusion models learn complex data distributions through a pair of processes: a forward process, where Gaussian noise is incrementally added to data until the data distribution converges to white noise, and a backward process, where a denoising neural network learns to reverse this noise corruption. The denoising neural network estimates the so-called score function, which is the gradient of the log-probability density of the (corrupted) data distribution
(
30
;
59
;
61
)
. The score function is central to understanding the learned distribution and to the generation of new data samples.

While diffusion models have demonstrated their ability to generate high-quality samples in high-dimensional settings, this success challenges the common expectation that learning in such environments should be hampered by the curse of dimensionality
(
67
;
70
)
. A plausible explanation for this success lies in the observation that many real-world datasets exhibit an underlying low-dimensional structure. Although the data may reside in high-dimensional ambient spaces, the intrinsic dimensionality of the data is often much lower, and these low-dimensional structures can often be understood as manifolds
(
66
;
55
)
.

In many domains, data display regularities and symmetries that suggest they can be modeled on low-dimensional manifolds. For instance, the set of all images of an object taken under different lighting conditions or orientations tends to lie on a low-dimensional manifold within the high-dimensional pixel space. By exploiting such low-dimensional structures, a line of work has shown that neural networks can efficiently approximate and estimate functions supported on these data domains, with performance dependent primarily on the intrinsic dimensionality of the data rather than its ambient dimensionality
(
57
;
63
;
11
;
62
;
69
)
.

While the low-dimensional manifold hypothesis offers an intuitive explanation for the empirical success of diffusion models, fundamental gaps persist in understanding their behavior on manifold data. Rigorous analyses and precise characterizations of statistical complexity—beyond idealized settings such as access to accurate score estimators
(
18
;
32
)
—remain underdeveloped. Notable sample complexity bounds are only established in a few very recent works
(
64
;
5
;
72
;
chakraborty2026generalizationpropertiesscorematchingdiffusion
)
. Yet, in these works, the interplay between the manifold geometry (e.g., curvature and boundary effects) and the performance of diffusion models is largely uncharacterized; see a detailed discussion in Section
1.2
. Despite diffusion models excelling at generating globally coherent structures, they often struggle with local fine-scale details. For instance, diffusion models frequently produce globally coherent images with local geometric inconsistencies—such as distorted fingers in synthesized human hands or hallucinated text in generated documents
(
46
;
47
;
2
;
56
)
. These challenges motivate two foundational questions.

Q1
:
Can diffusion models efficiently model manifold data, and how does their sample complexity scale with intrinsic (as opposed to ambient) dimensionality?

Q2
:
How do geometric properties—such as curvature—affect the quality of generative performance?

1.1
Contribution

In this paper, we develop a formal statistical framework for diffusion models in the context of data supported on unknown low-dimensional manifolds. We answer the two questions by viewing diffusion models as an unsupervised learner aiming to estimate the underlying data distribution. Different from traditional density estimation methods, diffusion models implicitly learn the distribution by estimating the score function, which presents significant challenges to the analysis. One key issue is that the score function can be highly irregular, even if the manifold and the data distribution are relatively smooth. In particular, it is well-known that the score function has an exploding magnitude as the backward diffusion process approaches zero noise
(
59
;
35
;
53
)
. To address this challenge, we propose a novel score decomposition approach, which projects the score according to the noise level, inspired by prior work on modeling data distributions on low-dimensional subspaces
(
52
;
10
)
.

Our contributions are three-fold: (1) we identify key intrinsic structures of score functions for manifold data under both the large noise (Lemma
3.1
) and small noise (Lemma
3.2
) regimes; (2) based on the structures, we construct suitable neural network architectures that can effectively approximate the score function in diffusion models (Theorem
4.4
), where we highlight the influence of the curvature of manifold; and (3) we provide statistical rates for estimating the score function and further the data distribution (Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
). The convergence rates depend on the intrinsic dimension of the data as well as the curvature of the manifold. We summarize our main theoretical results in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1

(Informal)
.

Under some manifold assumptions,

1.

the score function exhibits distinct decompositions in the large noise and small noise regimes: in the large-noise regime, it decomposes as a weighted sum of localized components that guide noisy data toward the manifold; in the small-noise regime, it decomposes via direct projection onto the manifold, with an additional interaction term that reflects the influence of curvature;

2.

based on the decompositions, the score function can be approximated by a ReLU neural network with

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-d/\beta})

non-zero weights, where
\epsilon
is the target approximation error.
The network size scales exponentially only in the intrinsic dimension
d
, while the dependence on the ambient dimension
D
is polynomial with a degree governed by the manifold intrinsic dimension and curvature.

3.

furthermore,
the data distribution is estimated in terms of Wasserstein distance at a rate

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-\frac{\beta+1}{d+2\beta}}),

where
n
is the sample size.

Our results provide crucial insights into how diffusion models capture low-dimensional data structures. While scaling up models and data sets has driven progress in data distribution modeling
(
34
;
29
)
, such approaches can be inefficient if they overlook the underlying structure of the data. For instance, Stable Diffusion employs a pre-trained variational autoencoder to first extract key features and reduce dimensionality before applying diffusion, demonstrating the value of architecture adaptation
(
21
)
. Through our theoretical framework—encompassing approximation and estimation—we emphasize the need to tailor diffusion models to unknown low-dimensional structures, thereby tightening approximation and estimation bounds. This work paves the way for more efficient architectures and training strategies that explicitly leverage these structures.

1.2
Related Work

Recent theoretical studies have framed diffusion models as unsupervised distribution learners and samplers, establishing their statistical distribution learning guarantees
(
52
;
10
;
20
;
48
)
and sampling convergence guarantees
(
7
;
15
;
39
;
14
;
6
;
43
;
42
)
. Such results offer invaluable theoretical insights into the efficiency and accuracy of diffusion models for modeling complex data.
Specifically,
52
analyzes
L_{2}
-error bounds in score estimation through score matching with neural networks, showing that the learned distribution achieves minimax optimal rates.

10
establishes explicit convergence rates when data reside on
low-dimensional linear subspaces.
48
interprets neural networks in diffusion models as denoising mechanisms, enabling an efficient score approximation in graphical models.

Moreover, several works have investigated diffusion models when data are supported on manifolds.
18
makes the first attempt to analyze diffusion models for learning low-dimensional manifold data. Assuming an accurate score estimator,
18
provides distribution estimation
guarantees of diffusion models in terms of the Wasserstein distance, though the obtained convergence rate
has an exponential dependence on the manifold diameter.
32

establishes a sampling theory for diffusion models on manifold data, with sampling speed scaling nearly linearly with the intrinsic data dimension.

64
proves a convergence rate of the distribution estimator induced by diffusion models only depends on the intrinsic data dimension. However, as
72
observes, the hidden constants in
64
’s convergence rates depend exponentially on
the ambient dimension
D
, which makes the derived rate ineffective when the ambient dimension is of logarithm order of sample size.
5
improves the

D
-dependence to polynomial order, but without characterizing how convergence rates depend on manifold curvature.
72
studies denoising scoring matching under relaxed manifold assumptions, considering noisy samples from a single-chart low-dimensional manifold.
Additionally,
43
show that the DDPM sampler adapts to unknown low-dimensional structure with iteration complexity scaling in the intrinsic dimension.
44
reveal a separation between learning the data geometry and the data distribution from a non-statistical learning perspective. They show that recovering the data distribution demands significantly more accurate score function than recovering the data manifold.
23
study the influence of manifold curvature on distribution approximation via diffusion models, establishing manifold-adaptive rates for a smoothed score function, though without sample complexity bounds.
Beyond the manifold setting,
chakraborty2026generalizationpropertiesscorematchingdiffusion
establish Wasserstein-
p
convergence rates for diffusion models that scale with a notion of Wasserstein dimension, a covering-number-based complexity measure generalizing the Minkowski dimension.

Our work addresses a fundamental gap in these theoretical analyses by explicitly characterizing how geometric properties of the data manifold—particularly its reach—govern both the efficiency of score estimation and subsequent distribution recovery guaranties. This geometric perspective reveals previously unexplored connections between manifold curvature and the performance of diffusion models.

Notation

Given a real value
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
\lfloor a\rfloor
as the largest integer smaller than
a
. For vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote
\|v\|
as its Euclidean norm and
\left\langle u,v\right\rangle=u^{\top}v
as the inner product. Given an arbitrary integer
k\geq 1
, we denote
v^{\otimes k}
as the
k
-th order outer product of
v
, which is a tensor with
[v^{\otimes k}]_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}v_{j_{i}}
. Given a multi-index
\xi=[\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{N}^{D}
, we denote
v^{\xi}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{\xi_{i}}
.
We denote
\mathcal{B}^{d}(v,r)=\{x:\|x-v\|\leq r\}
as a
d
-dimensional Euclidean ball, where
v
is the center and
r
is the radius. We frequently invoke two operations on a set
A
: For a scalar
a>0
, we denote
aA=\{ax:x\in A\}
and for a mapping
f
, we denote
f(A)=\{f(x):x\in A\}
. Given a real-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and a multi-index
\xi\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we denote
\partial^{\xi}f
as the partial derivative
\frac{\partial^{|\xi|}f}{\partial x_{1}^{\xi_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{\xi_{d}}}
with
|\xi|=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\xi_{i}
. Given a distribution
P
, we denote the
L^{2}(P)
-norm of
f
as
\|f\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}=\int f^{2}(x)\textrm{d}P(x)
.

2
Preliminary: Smooth Manifold and Diffusion Model

This section provides a brief introduction to manifolds and diffusion models. Readers may refer to
68
;
25
for a comprehensive introduction to manifolds and
9
;
13
;
65
for a technical exposure of diffusion models.

2.1
Riemannian Manifold and Density

We denote
\mathcal{M}
as a
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
with
d\ll D
. On a coarse level, a manifold
\mathcal{M}
is a set which is locally Euclidean, meaning that a small local neighborhood on
\mathcal{M}
can be continuously mapped into a Euclidean space. This is formalized with the definition of
charts
.

Definition 2.1

(Chart)
.

A chart for
\mathcal{M}
is a pair
(U,\phi)
such that
U\subset\mathcal{M}
is open and
\phi:U\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d},
where
\phi
is a homeomorphism (i.e., bijective,
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
are both continuous).

A manifold typically consists of many local neighborhoods overlapping with each other. We say two charts
(U,\phi)
and
(V,\psi)
on
\mathcal{M}
are
C^{\infty}
compatible if and only if the transition functions,

\phi\circ\psi^{-1}:\psi(U\cap V)\mapsto\phi(U\cap V)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\psi\circ\phi^{-1}:\phi(U\cap V)\mapsto\psi(U\cap V),

are both
C^{\infty}
.

Definition 2.2

(
C^{\infty}
Atlas)
.

A
C^{\infty}
atlas for
\mathcal{M}
is a collection of pairwise
C^{\infty}
compatible charts
\{(U_{k},\phi_{k})\}_{k\in\mathcal{A}}
such that
\bigcup_{k\in\mathcal{A}}U_{k}=\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 2.3

(Smooth Manifold)
.

A smooth manifold is a manifold together with a
C^{\infty}
atlas.

Classical examples of smooth manifolds are the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, the torus, and the unit sphere. We further define a Riemannian manifold as a pair
(\mathcal{M},g)
, where
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth manifold and
g
is a Riemannian metric
(
40
, Chapter 2)
. An
isometric embedding
of the
d
-dimensional
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
is an embedding that preserves the Riemannian metric of
\mathcal{M}
. For more rigorous statements, see the classic reference
(
25
)
.

2.1.1
Exponential Map

In this paper, we will frequently work with a common choice of atlas on a Riemannian manifold, constructed from exponential maps. Given a point
x\in\mathcal{M}
, we denote
T_{x}\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
as the tangent space of
\mathcal{M}
at
x
; a formal definition is provided in
(
68
, Section 8.1)
. The tangent space
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
is a
d
-dimensional vector space and isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Therefore, we can identify vectors in
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
with their
d
-dimensional representations, which we denote as the space
T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We refer to
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
and
T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}
both as tangent spaces.

To define exponential maps, we begin with generalizing straight lines in Euclidean spaces to manifolds. We denote the Riemannian distance
d_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on
\mathcal{M}
as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,y)=\inf\{{\sf length}(\gamma)~|~\gamma\textup{ is a }C^{1}(\mathcal{M})\textup{ curve such that }\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(1)=y\},

where
{\sf length}(\gamma)
is the length of curve
\gamma
computed with respect to the Riemannian metric
g
. Riemannian distance
d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,y)
is referred to as the length of the shortest path or
geodesic
connecting
x
and
y
on the manifold. Exponential map builds upon geodesics.

Definition 2.4

(Exponential map)
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{M}
. For a tangent vector
v\in T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}
, there is a unique geodesic
\gamma
that starts at
x
with initial velocity
v
, i.e.,
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=v
. The exponential map centered at
x
is given by
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(v)=\gamma(1)
, for all
v\in T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}
.

As can be seen, the exponential map takes a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
on the tangent space
T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}
as input. The output,
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(v)\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, is the point on the manifold obtained by traveling along a (unit speed) geodesic curve that starts at
x
and has initial direction
v
; see Figure
1
for an illustration.

It is known that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
, there exists a radius
r
such that the exponential map restricted to a ball
\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)
is a diffeomorphism onto its image, i.e., it is a smooth map with smooth inverse. As the sufficiently small
r
-ball in the tangent space may vary for each
x\in\mathcal{M}
, we define the injectivity radius of
\mathcal{M}
as the minimum
r
over all
x\in\mathcal{M}
.

Figure 1:
Demonstration of tangent space
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
, geodesic, and exponential map based on
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
v\in T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 2.5

(Injectivity radius)
.

For all
x\in\mathcal{M}
, the injectivity radius at
x
is defined as
{\tt inj}_{\mathcal{M}}(x)=\sup~\{r>0\mid\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}:\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)\mapsto\mathcal{M}\textup{ is a diffeomorphism}\}
. Then the injectivity radius of
\mathcal{M}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\tt inj}(\mathcal{M})=\inf~\{{\tt inj}_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\mid x\in\mathcal{M}\}.

As a result, for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
, the exponential map restricted to a ball of radius
{\tt inj}(\mathcal{M})
in
T^{d}_{x}\mathcal{M}
is a well-defined diffeomorphism, which validates
(U_{x},\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}^{-1})
as a chart when
U_{x}=\{\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(v)\mid v\in\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,{\tt inj}(\mathcal{M}))\}
.
It is convenient to denote the inverse of the exponential map as
\mathrm{\mathbb{Log}}_{x}
, the log map.
Controlling a quantity called reach allows us to lower bound the injectivity radius.

Definition 2.6

(Reach
(
24
)
)
.

The reach
\tau
of a manifold
\mathcal{M}
is defined as the quantity

\displaystyle\tau=\inf~\{r>0\mid\exists~x\neq y\in\mathcal{M},v\in\mathbb{R}^{D}\text{ such that }r=\|x-v\|=\|y-v\|=\inf_{z\in\mathcal{M}}\|z-v\|\}.

Intuitively, if the distance of a point
x
to
\mathcal{M}
is smaller than the reach, then there is a unique point in
\mathcal{M}
that is closest to
x
. However, if the distance between
x
and
\mathcal{M}
is larger than the reach, then there will no longer be a unique closest point to
x
in
\mathcal{M}
. For example, the reach of a sphere is its radius. The reach gives us control over the injectivity radius
{\tt inj}({\mathcal{M}})
.

Proposition 2.7

(Proof in
1
)
.

For a manifold
\mathcal{M}
with reach
\tau>0
, it holds that
{\tt inj}({\mathcal{M}})\geq\pi\tau
.

Remark 2.8

(Atlas using exponential map)
.

Constructing an atlas on a compact Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{M}
using exponential maps is commonly adopted. In particular, recall that for any point
x\in\mathcal{M}
, the exponential map is diffeomorphic on a ball
\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)
with
r<{\tt inj}(\mathcal{M})
. Denote
U_{x}=\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,r))
as a local neighborhood, whose union
\cup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}U_{x}
forms an open cover of
\mathcal{M}
. For a compact
\mathcal{M}
, there exists a finite sub-cover
\{U_{x_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}
. As shown in
11
,
C_{\mathcal{M}}
depends on the reach and dimension of the manifold. We suppress the subscript
x_{k}
to
k
and thus,
\{(U_{k},\mathrm{\mathbb{Log}}_{k})\}_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}
becomes an atlas on
\mathcal{M}
.

2.1.2
Differentiable Function on Manifold

The existence of an atlas allows us to define differentiable functions on
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 2.9

(
C^{s}
Functions on
\mathcal{M}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. A function
f:\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is
C^{s}
if for any chart
(U,\phi)
, the composition
f\circ\phi^{-1}:\phi(U)\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable up to order
s
.

We note that the definition of
C^{s}
functions is independent of the choice of the chart
(U,\phi)
. Suppose
(V,\psi)
is another chart and
V\cap U\neq\emptyset
. Then we have
f\circ\psi^{-1}=(f\circ\phi^{-1})\circ(\phi\circ\psi^{-1})
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth manifold,
(U,\phi)
and
(V,\psi)
are
C^{\infty}
compatible. Thus,
f\circ\phi^{-1}
is
C^{s}
and
\phi\circ\psi^{-1}
is
C^{\infty}
, and their composition is
C^{s}
. The following definition generalizes
C^{s}
functions to Hölder continuous functions.

Definition 2.10

(Hölder Functions on
\mathcal{M}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
d
-dimensional compact Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
.
Let
\{(U_{k},\textbf{Log}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathcal{A}}
be an atlas of
\mathcal{M}
where
\textbf{Log}_{k}
is the log map on
U_{k}
.
For
\beta>0
, the Hölder norm of a function
f:\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(\mathcal{M})}:=\max\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathcal{A},\;u\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\\
|u|\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in U_{k}}\big|\partial^{u}(f\circ\textbf{Exp}_{k})\big|,

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathcal{A},\;u\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\\
|u|=\lfloor\beta\rfloor\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in U_{k}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\Big|\partial^{u}(f\circ\textbf{Exp}_{k})\big|_{\textbf{Log}_{k}(x)}-\partial^{u}(f\circ\textbf{Exp}_{k})\big|_{\textbf{Log}_{k}(y)}\Big|}{\|\textbf{Log}_{k}(x)-\textbf{Log}_{k}(y)\|^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}}\Bigg\}.

A function
f
is
\beta
-Hölder continuous if
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(\mathcal{M})}<\infty
.
We further denote
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(\mathcal{M},C_{\mathcal{H}})
as the collection of
\beta
-Hölder functions on
\mathcal{M}
with Hölder norm bounded by a constant
C_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Definition
2.10
requires that all
\lfloor\beta\rfloor
-th order derivatives of
f\circ\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}
are Hölder continuous.
We recover the standard Hölder class on a Euclidean space when
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}
is the identity map. We next introduce the partition of unity, which plays a crucial role in our construction of neural networks.

Definition 2.11

(Partition of Unity, Definition 13.4 in
68
)
.

A
C^{\infty}
partition of unity on a manifold
\mathcal{M}
is a collection of nonnegative
C^{\infty}
functions
\rho_{k}:\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
for
k\geq 1
such that

1.

the collection of supports,
\{\textrm{supp}(\rho_{k})\}_{k\in\mathcal{A}}
, is locally finite, i.e., every point on
\mathcal{M}
has a neighborhood that meets only finitely many of
{\rm supp}(\rho_{k})
’s;

2.

\sum_{k}\rho_{k}=1
.

For a smooth manifold, a
C^{\infty}
partition of unity always exists.

Proposition 2.12

(Existence of a
C^{\infty}
partition of unity, Theorem 13.7 in
68
)
.

Let
\{U_{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{A}}
be an open cover of a compact smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
. Then there is a
C^{\infty}
partition of unity
\{\rho_{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{A}}
where every
\rho_{k}
has a compact support such that
\textrm{supp}(\rho_{k})\subset U_{k}
.

2.1.3
Distribution and Density on Manifold

To define distributions and density functions on
\mathcal{M}
, we first define the
volume measure
on a manifold
\mathcal{M}
and establish integration.

Definition 2.13

(Volume measure)
.

For a compact
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{M}
, its volume measure
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
is the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
\mathcal{M}

(
24
)
.

We say that a data distribution
P_{\rm data}
supported on
\mathcal{M}
has a density
p_{\rm data}
if the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
P_{\rm data}
with respect to
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
is
p_{\rm data}
.
According to
22
, for any continuous function
f:\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
supported within the image of the ball
\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)
under the exponential map for
r<{\tt inj}(\mathcal{M})
, we have

\displaystyle\int f{\rm d}P_{\rm data}=\int(f\cdot p_{\rm data}){\rm d}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=\int_{\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)}(f\cdot p_{\rm data})\circ\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(v)G_{x}(v){\rm d}v.

(2.1)

Here
G_{x}(v)=\sqrt{\det g_{ij}^{x}(v)}
with
g_{ij}^{x}(v)=\langle\partial\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(v)/\partial e_{i},\partial\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}(v)/\partial e_{j}\rangle_{g}
for
(e_{1},...,e_{d})
being an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
.

To extend the integration of
f
to arbitrary domains, we consider a partition of unity
\{\rho_{k}(x)\}_{k\in\mathcal{A}}
, such that each
\rho_{k}(x)
is supported on the image of the ball
\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x_{k}}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)
under the exponential map. Then we have

\displaystyle\int f{\rm d}P_{\rm data}=\int(f\cdot p_{\rm data}){\rm d}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{k}\int_{\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x_{k}}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)}(f\cdot p_{\rm data}\cdot\rho_{k})\circ\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x_{k}}(v)G_{x_{k}}(v){\rm d}v.

(2.2)

Remark 2.14

(Decomposition of Manifold Distribution)
.

An important implication of (
2.2
) is that we may view the manifold distribution
P_{\rm data}
as a mixture of local measures supported on charts. To see this, for any measurable subset
A\subset\mathcal{M}
, we define
P_{\textrm{data},k}(A)=\int(\mathds{1}_{A}\cdot p_{\rm data}\cdot\rho_{k})\circ\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x_{k}}(v)G_{x_{k}}(v){\rm d}v
. Then it holds that
P_{\rm data}(A)=\sum_{k}P_{\textrm{data},k}(A)
. Clearly, each
P_{\textrm{data},k}
is locally supported within the image of
\mathcal{B}^{d}_{T_{x_{k}}\mathcal{M}}(0,r)
in the tangent space. Since the support of
P_{\textrm{data},k}
may overlap with each other,
P_{\textrm{data},k}
is only a measure instead of a probability distribution with unit total mass. For convenience, we denote the normalized version of
P_{\textrm{data},k}
as
\mu_{k}
, which is a probability distribution supported on
U_{k}
. Then it holds that
P_{\rm data}=\sum_{k}P_{\textrm{data},k}(U_{k})\cdot\mu_{k}
, where
P_{\textrm{data},k}(U_{k})
is the total mass of
\mu_{k}
. Such a decomposition into local measures is crucial to our analysis on structures in the score function in Section
3
.

2.2
Diffusion Model

We adopt a continuous-time description of diffusion models for the ease of theoretical analysis. There are two coupled processes in diffusion models. The forward process progressively injects independent Gaussian noise to clean data. Following the convention in literature, we use an Ornstein-Ulhenbeck process (also known as the variance preserving process):

\displaystyle\textrm{d}X_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}X_{t}\textrm{d}t+\textrm{d}B_{t}\quad\text{with}\quad X_{0}\sim P_{\rm data},

(2.3)

where
(B_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Wiener process. We denote the marginal distribution of
X_{t}
at time
t
as
P_{t}
. Under (
2.3
), given
X_{0}
, the conditional distribution of
X_{t}|X_{0}
is Gaussian
{\sf N}(\alpha_{t}X_{0},h_{t}I_{D})
, where
\alpha_{t}=\exp(-t/2)
and
h_{t}=1-\alpha^{2}_{t}
. Consequently, under mild conditions, (
2.3
) transforms the initial distribution
P_{\rm data}
to
P_{\infty}={\sf N}(0,I_{D})
at infinite time. In practice, the forward process (
2.3
) will terminate at a sufficiently large time
T>0
, where the corrupted marginal distribution
P_{T}
is expected to be close to the standard Gaussian distribution.

When generating new samples, diffusion models reverse the time of (
2.3
), which leads to the following backward process,

\displaystyle\textrm{d}Y_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{2}Y_{t}+\nabla\log p_{T-t}(Y_{t})\right]\textrm{d}t+\textrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}\quad\text{with}\quad Y_{0}\sim P_{T},

(2.4)

where
\nabla\log p_{t}(\cdot)
is the so-called score function, i.e., the gradient of log probability density function of
P_{t}
, and
\overline{B}_{t}
is another independent Wiener process. With mild assumptions, the backward process
(Y_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
has the same distribution as the time-reversed version of the forward process
(X_{T-t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

(
3
;
28
)
.

Simulating (
2.4
), however, poses substantial difficulties, since both the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}
and initial distribution
P_{T}
are unknown. To address these challenges, we replace
P_{T}
by the standard Gaussian distribution. Moreover, we use a score estimator
\widehat{s}
instead of the ground truth score
\nabla\log p_{t}
. The estimated score
\widehat{s}
is parameterized by a neural network. With these substitutions, we obtain the following backward SDE,

\displaystyle\textrm{d}\widehat{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Y}_{t}+\widehat{s}(\widehat{Y}_{t},T-t)\right]\textrm{d}t+\textrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{Y}_{0}\sim{\sf N}(0,I_{D}).

(2.5)

In practice, new data are generated by applying a discretization scheme to simulate (
2.5
).

The estimated score
\widehat{s}
is obtained by score matching
(
59
;
30
)
, which solves a least square problem aggregated over time
t\leq T
. Conceptually, we minimize

\displaystyle\min_{s\in\mathcal{F}}~\mathcal{R}(s)=\frac{1}{T-t_{0}}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim P_{\rm data}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{t}\sim{\sf N}(\alpha_{t}X_{0},h_{t}I)}\left[\left\|s(X_{t},t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(X_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]{\,\mathrm{d}}t,

where
\mathcal{F}
is a class of score functions and
t_{0}
is an early-stopping time for stabilizing the training
(
59
;
60
)
.
Since
\nabla\log p_{t}
is not tractable with unknown
P_{\rm data}
, loss
\mathcal{R}
cannot be directly implemented and optimized. Thanks to the seminal work
(
33
)
, an equivalent loss (up to a constant) is derived:

\displaystyle\min_{s\in\mathcal{F}}~\mathcal{L}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim P_{\rm data}}[\ell(X_{0};s)]\quad\text{with}

\displaystyle\ell(X_{0};s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{T}-t_{0}}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{X_{t}\sim{\sf N}(\alpha_{t}X_{0},h_{t}I)}\Big\|s(X_{t},t)+\frac{X_{t}-\alpha_{t}X_{0}}{h_{t}}\Big\|^{2}{\,\mathrm{d}}t.

It is shown that
\nabla\mathcal{R}(s)=\nabla\mathcal{L}(s)
for any
s
, and therefore, the minimizer of
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{L}
is identical. Given a data set
\mathcal{D}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
sampled from
P_{\rm data}
, we replace the population expectation by an empirical average:

\displaystyle\widehat{s}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{s\in\mathcal{F}}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(s)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(x_{i};s).

(2.6)

In our analysis, we choose
\mathcal{F}
as a feedforward neural network architecture (Section
4
) taking
X_{t}
and time
t
as input. For technical convenience, we assume
\alpha_{t}
and
h_{t}
are also inputs to the score network, since they are pre-determined in the forward process. We also focus on the empirical risk minimizer in (
2.6
).

3
Structure in Score Function for Manifold Data

The score function
\nabla\log p_{t}
is the steering power of diffusion models in generating high-fidelity samples.
In this section, we study structures in the score function, when the ground-truth data distribution
P_{\rm data}
is supported on a
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{M}
. We present key insights on how the original manifold structure and data distribution are progressively restored from pure noise in the backward dynamics, driven by the score function. Formally, we show that the score function decomposes distinctively at different stages of the backward process. These properties inform an efficient representation and estimation of the score function.

3.1
Warmup: Linear Subspace Data

We start with a simple yet intriguing example—data concentrated on a linear subspace. Consider data
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
being represented as
x=Az
, where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times d}
is an unknown matrix with orthonormal columns and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a latent variable following some underlying distribution. This says that each data point lies in the subspace spanned by the column vectors of matrix
A
. For any
t>0
, the score function decomposes into two orthogonal components as

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{t}(x)=\underbrace{\frac{\alpha_{t}\mathbb{E}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]-\Pi_{A}(x)}{h_{t}}}_{s_{A}(x,t):~\text{on-support~score}}\underbrace{-\frac{x-\Pi_{A}(x)}{h_{t}}}_{s_{\bot}(x,t):~\rm orthogonal~score},

(3.1)

where
\Pi_{A}(x)=AA^{\top}x
is the projection onto the column span of matrix
A
.
The same result is presented in
10
with an explicit formula for the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]
using the latent variable. We term the two components as the on-support score
s_{A}
and the orthogonal score
s_{\bot}
, respectively. The on-support score
s_{A}
lies in the column space of matrix
A
as
\mathbb{E}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]
concentrated on the subspace. The orthogonal score function
s_{\perp}(x,t)=-\frac{1}{h_{t}}(I-AA^{\top})x
is perpendicular to the subspace. As time
t\to 0
, the variance
h_{t}
of the added noise tends to zero, and we check that
s_{\bot}
blows up. This is consistent with the score blowup issue for learning generic structured data
59
;
35
;
53
.

The score decomposition suggests decoupled dynamics in the backward process on the subspace and its orthogonal complement. The on-support score
s_{A}
aims to recover the distribution of the latent variable, while the orthogonal score
s_{\perp}
dictates a contraction towards the subspace whenever the noisy state
x
is not on the subspace. As
t\to 0
, the strength of the contraction to the subspace amplifies and finally pushes data onto the subspace in the limit.

Structures in the score function motivate an efficient statistical argument to demystify the empirical performance of diffusion models in complex high-dimensional data
52
;
10
. However, linear subspace is oversimplified for real-world applications, spelling the need for a generalization to generic geometric data, which we present in the next section.

3.2
Nonlinear Manifold Data

We generalize the decomposition in Section
3.1
to compact
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds. On the one hand, the score function for a manifold
\mathcal{M}
presents local structures analogous to that for the linear subspace case, given that manifolds locally resemble Euclidean space. On the other hand, the score function possesses time-inhomogeneous behavior, and the score decomposition becomes more involved due to nonlinearity.

We investigate structures of the score function in two stages depending on the amount of noise injected in data. We refer to “
large noise
” when
t
is large in the forward process (
2.3
), and “
small noise
” when
t
is small. The forward process suppresses the data magnitude exponentially fast, i.e.,
\alpha_{t}=e^{-t/2}
, therefore, in the large noise regime, injected noise dominates the corrupted data. The behavior of the score function centers around pushing noisy data towards a close vicinity of the original data manifold. On the other hand, in the small noise regime, the score function recovers geometric details of the manifold and eventually recovers the clean data distribution. Figure
2
demonstrates the distinct behaviors in two regimes. A two-time-scale perspective also appears in
20
. We formalize our analysis in the sequel.

3.2.1
Large Noise: Weighted Decomposition

Given a large time
t
, due to the dominance of the injected noise, it suffices to consider a coarse linear approximation to the manifold
\mathcal{M}
constructed by the tangent spaces at
x_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,C_{\mathcal{M}}
.
For the
k
-th chart, we denote the projection of
x
onto the shrunk tangent space
\alpha_{t}\cdot T_{x_{k}}\mathcal{M}
as

\displaystyle\Pi_{k}(x,t)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{y\in\alpha_{t}\cdot T_{x_{k}}\mathcal{M}}\|y-x\|\quad\text{for}\quad k=1,\ldots,C_{\mathcal{M}}.

We recall that
\alpha_{t}
is the shrinkage ratio introduced by the forward process (
2.3
) at time
t
. Based on projections onto tangent spaces and the decomposition of manifold distribution into a mixture of local measures in Remark
2.14
, we present the following weighted score decomposition, whose proof is provided in Appendix
A.1
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For a large time
t>0
and an arbitrary noisy state
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(x)
takes the form:

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{t}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}w_{k}(x,t)\bigg(\underbrace{\frac{\alpha_{t}\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim\mu_{k}}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]-\Pi_{k}(x,t)}{h_{t}}}_{s^{k}_{\mathcal{M}}:~\text{$k$-th~on-support~score}}~~\underbrace{-\frac{x-\Pi_{k}(x,t)}{h_{t}}}_{s^{k}_{\bot}:~\text{$k$-th~orthogonal~score}}\bigg),

where

\displaystyle w_{k}(x,t)=\frac{\int\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\textrm{data},k}(x_{0})}{\sum_{j=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\int\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\textrm{data},j}(x_{0})}.

Lemma
3.1
decomposes the score function according to the atlas on
\mathcal{M}
, giving rise to a weighted sum of localized terms. We provide interpretation of these terms in below.

Decomposition on Single Chart

For the
k
-th chart,
s_{\perp}^{k}
acts analogously to the orthogonal score in the linear subspace case. While the difference is that
\Pi_{k}(x,t)
is a time-dependent projection onto the rescaled tangent space. As a result, the noisy state
x
moves towards the tangent space
T_{x_{k}}\mathcal{M}
and simultaneously approaches the manifold. Since
\Pi_{k}(x,t)
may not belong to the manifold, the on-support term
s_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}
guides the noisy state moving towards
U_{k}
by compensating for the deviation of
\Pi_{k}(x,t)
from the anticipated mean
\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim\mu_{k}}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]
. We explain
w_{k}
and
\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim\mu_{k}}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]
in more detail.

Understanding Weight
w_{k}

Recall the decomposition of
P_{\rm data}
into local measures on each chart. The weight
w_{k}
is interpreted as the probability that a given noisy state
x
eventually falls into the
k
-th chart. If
x
is close to the scaled chart
\alpha_{t}U_{k}
, the score function is dominated by the corresponding chart. That is,
w_{k}\approx 1
and the score function behaves as if there is only one chart on the manifold. On the contrary, when
x
is not close to any of
\alpha_{t}U_{k}
, the score function behaves as that of a mixture of distributions. Attentive readers may find
w_{k}
echos that in Gaussian mixture models. In fact, for a Gaussian mixture
\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\pi_{k}{\sf N}(\mu_{k},I)
with prior probability
\pi_{k}
, the score function is
\nabla\log p_{t}(x)=-\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}c_{k}(x,t)(x-\alpha_{t}\mu_{k})
, where
c_{k}(x,t)=\pi_{k}\exp(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}\mu_{k}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}})/\sum_{j=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\pi_{j}\exp(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}\mu_{j}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}})
. Our localized measure
P_{\textrm{data},k}
generalizes the prior probability.

Anticipated Mean
\mathbb{E}_{X_{0}\sim\mu_{k}}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]

The anticipated mean can be interpreted as a posterior average, where the prior distribution is
\mu_{k}
and the current observation is
X_{t}=x
. Equivalently, we presume that the noisy state generates clean data belonging to
U_{k}
. Unfortunately, the anticipated mean is generally not on the manifold or the tangent space due to nonlinearity, leaving
s_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}
and
s_{\perp}^{k}
not orthogonal to each other.

3.2.2
Small Noise: Local Decomposition

Driven by the score function in the large noise regime, noisy states gradually reduce noise and evolve towards a close vicinity of the manifold. Then the small noise regime emerges, since the coarse piecewise linear approximation using tangent spaces becomes insufficient for precise manifold recovery. Instead, the score function operates with higher precision, accurately capturing the local geometry of
\mathcal{M}
around
x
through orthogonal projection directly onto
\mathcal{M}
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
has a positive reach
\tau
. We define an inflated region around
\mathcal{M}
as
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{B}(x,\tau)
. At time
t
, for any
x\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
, we define

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{y\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{M}}\|y-x\|.

(3.2)

The projection (
3.2
) is well-defined as shown in
41
. The following decomposition of the score function is established using the projection operator, whose proof is provided in Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any fixed time
t>0
, given an arbitrary
x\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
, the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(x)
decomposes into

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{t}(x)=\underbrace{\frac{\alpha_{t}\mathbb{E}[X_{0}|X_{t}=x]-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)}{h_{t}}}_{s_{\mathcal{M}}:~\text{on-support score}}~~\underbrace{-\frac{x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)}{h_{t}}}_{s_{\perp}:~\text{orthogonal score}}.

Moreover, for the on-support score, it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 32.52127pts_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)=\nabla_{x}\log\left[\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}(E_{1}(t)+E_{2}(t))\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\rm data}(x_{0})\right]\quad\text{with}

\displaystyle E_{1}(t)=\|\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad E_{2}(t)=2\langle x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t),\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\rangle.

Compared to the large noise regime in Lemma
3.1
, small noise decomposition does not assert a mixed structure. Alternatively, the decomposition is driven by the unique projection
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)
onto the manifold, resembling that in the linear subspace case. The reason behind this is that the variance of the added noise to state
x
is small. It is less likely that
x
is obtained by corrupting distant clean data points on the manifold. We provide the following further discussions.

Hitting
\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
with High Probability

Conditioned on a clean data point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
, the distribution of
X_{t}
is Gaussian
{\sf N}(\alpha_{t}x_{0},h_{t}I)
. When
t
is sufficiently small, it holds that
\alpha_{t}\approx 1
and
h_{t}\approx 0
. Therefore, we have
\sqrt{Dh_{t}}<\alpha_{t}\tau
, meaning that
\|X_{t}-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|<\alpha_{t}\tau
with high probability. Consequently,
\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
is hit by noisy states with high probability in the forward process (
2.3
). More importantly, when
\tau
is small,
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
is a narrow band around the manifold, making the requirement on small
t
stringent. As a result, the learning of the score function becomes challenging. We present explicit approximation and estimation complexities of the score function in relation to the reach
\tau
in Section
4
.

Further Decomposition in
s_{\mathcal{M}}

While Lemma
3.2
replicates the same form of score decomposition as the linear subspace, the on-support score possesses intricate structures captured by
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. Term
E_{1}
attempts to distribute the probability mass at the projection point to the manifold according to the Gaussian transition kernel defined in (
2.3
). Unfortunately, the nonlinear manifold introduces a nuisance: the movement
x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)
is not orthogonal to the movement towards the projection
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}x_{0}
. As a result,
E_{2}
captures the interaction between the two movements, and we cannot decouple the dynamics in the backward process as in the linear subspace case. It is worth mentioning that the interaction term
E_{2}
heavily depends on the curvature of the manifold. As a sanity check, when the manifold reduces to a linear subspace without curvature,
E_{2}
vanishes and Lemma
3.2
reduces to (
3.1
). Figure
2
illustrates such an interaction.

Implication of Score Decomposition

The score decompositions in Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
are key to an efficient neural network approximation developed in Section
4
. The on-support scores map
D
-dimensional noisy states to points that can be effectively approximated using
d
-dimensional representations, since the manifold assumes low-dimensional local charts. The orthogonal score exhibits simple structures and can also be approximated efficiently.

Figure 2:
Score decomposition for linear subspace and general manifold. For linear subspace data, the projection
\Pi_{A}
is globally defined for all
t
and noisy state
x
. The score function decomposes into two orthogonal components. For a smooth manifold, the score function behaves distinctly according to the magnitude of the added noise. In the large noise regime, score function decomposes corresponding to tangent spaces on the manifold. In the small noise regime, local decomposition exists and is centered around the projection point
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x_{t_{2}},t_{2})
of a noisy state
x_{t_{2}}
.

4
Approximating Score Function using Neural Network

In practice, score functions are parameterized by deep neural networks. Leveraging the structures in score functions identified in Section
3
, we develop score approximation theories. We impose some mild regularity conditions on the manifold.

Assumption 4.1
(Data Domain).

\mathcal{M}
is a
d
-dimensional compact Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. There exists a constant
B_{\mathcal{M}}>0
such that, for any point
x\in\mathcal{M}
, it holds that
\|x\|_{\infty}\leq B_{\mathcal{M}}
. The reach of
\mathcal{M}
is lower bounded by
\tau>0
.

A positive reach of
\mathcal{M}
ensures geometric regularity by ruling out sharp corners, cusps, or self-intersections. This enables well-defined projections onto the manifold, and ensures that local charts behave nicely with bounded distortion and controlled curvature. Many commonly studied manifolds satisfy such regularity conditions, including the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
and the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Assumption 4.2
.

For the atlas in Remark
2.8
, there exists a constant
\eta\in(0,1]
such that choosing
r=3\tau
ensures
\mathcal{B}(x_{k},\eta\tau)\cap\mathcal{M}
is a subset of
U_{k}
.

Assumption
4.2
is an implicit regularity condition for the geometric structure of
\mathcal{M}
. While exponential map
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}
is well-defined on
\mathcal{B}_{T_{x}\mathcal{M}}(0,3\tau)
by Proposition
2.7
, Assumption
4.2
further guarantees its inverse map
\mathrm{\mathbb{Log}}_{x}
is well-defined so that
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}
is locally a bijection. In addition, we assume the smoothness of
the ground-truth probability density
p_{\rm data}
.

Assumption 4.3
(Data Distribution).

The data distribution
P_{\rm data}
has a density
p_{\rm data}
supported on
\mathcal{M}
and belonging to
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(\mathcal{M})
for a Hölder index
\beta>0
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C_{f}>1
such that
C_{f}^{-1}\leq p_{\rm data}(x)\leq C_{f}
for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
.

Assumption
4.3
generalizes the standard Hölder densities in nonparametric statistics
67
;
70
to Riemannian manifolds. We require the density bounded below by a positive constant for technical convenience, which is often adopted in the existing literature
67
;
52
.

We parameterize the score function using a class of deep neural networks
\mathcal{F}
with entrywise ReLU activation (i.e.,
{\rm ReLU}(a)=\max\{a,0\}
):

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(L,W,S,B,R):=\big\{f(x)&=A_{L}\cdot{{\rm ReLU}}(\cdots{{\rm ReLU}}(A_{1}x+b_{1})\cdots)+b_{L}:\\
&\text{width bounded by }W,~\sum_{i=1}^{L}(\|A_{i}\|_{0}+\|b_{i}\|_{0})\leq S,\\
&\max_{i}\|A_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq B,~\max_{i}\|b_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq B,~\sup_{z}\|f(z)\|_{2}\leq R\big\}.\end{split}

(4.1)

Here
\|\cdot\|_{0}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denote the number of nonzero entries and the maximum magnitude of entries. Hyper-parameters control the depth (
L
) and width (
W
) of the network, as well as the sparsity (
S
) and boundedness (
B
and
R
). The following theorem shows that the network class
\mathcal{F}
can accurately represent the score function.

Theorem 4.4
.

[Score Approximation Theory] Suppose Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
hold.
For any sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
and any time
t\in[t_{0},T]
with
t_{0}={\rm poly}(n^{-1})
and
T=\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\epsilon))
, there exists a network class
\mathcal{F}(L,W,S,B,R)
that yields a function
\bar{s}
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{s}(x,t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(x)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{t})}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{h_{t}}D^{2\gamma+d+2}\epsilon^{2}\right)\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma=\left\lceil\frac{\beta\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\beta\log\tau}\right\rceil.

The configuration of
\mathcal{F}
verifies

\displaystyle L=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\gamma^{3}\right),\quad W=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(D^{\gamma}\gamma^{3}\epsilon^{-d/\beta}\right),\quad S=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\gamma D^{\gamma}\epsilon^{-d/\beta}\right),\quad B=\mathcal{O}\left(h_{t_{0}}^{-\gamma}\epsilon^{-2\log(1/\epsilon)/\beta}\right).

The complete proof is provided in Appendix
B
, and a proof sketch is presented in Section
4.2
. The precise requirement on
\epsilon

is specified in condition (
B.39
).
Theorem
4.4
establishes an
L^{2}
score approximation guarantee over the unbounded domain
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, while existing universal approximation theory of neural networks majorly focuses on approximating target functions on a compact domain under the
L^{\infty}
norm
75
;
57
;
11
. Our analysis addresses the unboundedness through a truncation argument. Moreover, to achieve an
\epsilon
approximation error, the network size scales exponentially with the intrinsic dimension
d
, emphasizing the adaptability of neural networks to the manifold structures. Several additional remarks are in turn.

Influence of Manifold Curvature

Theorem
4.4
suggests that the approximation error needs to scale with the reach
\tau
to capture manifold structures. A slight rewrite of the error guarantee shows an explicit impact of the reach. For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\gamma>0
, the same network class
\mathcal{F}
in Theorem
4.4
gives rise to a
\bar{s}
with

\displaystyle\|\bar{s}(x,t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(x)\|_{L^{2}(P_{t})}^{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{D^{2\gamma+d+2}}{h_{t}}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2\gamma/\beta}}{\tau^{2\gamma}}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\right).

(4.2)

Setting
\tau^{-2\gamma}\epsilon^{2\gamma/\beta}=\epsilon^{2}
recovers the bound in Theorem
4.4
. As evident from (
4.2
), a smaller
\tau
, corresponding to higher manifold curvature, leads to increased approximation error. The reason behind this is that a manifold with a small
\tau
exhibits rapidly changing local geometry, causing local projections to possess significantly different orientations. These variations are difficult to approximate, especially in the small noise regime. Indeed,
E_{2}
in Lemma
3.2
varies significantly as noisy state
x
moves.

Dependence on Ambient Dimension

The approximation guarantee depends on the ambient dimension
D
and is determined by the exponent
\gamma
. When the reach
\tau\geq 1
, we can show that
\gamma
is upper bounded by
\beta
. This indicates that for relatively smoothly varying manifolds, the dependence on the ambient dimension scales at most
D^{2\beta+d+2}
—a polynomial dependence. On the other hand, when
\tau<1
,
\gamma
grows as
\tau
decreases, potentially leading to an exponential dependence on
D
for extremely small reach. Our bound improves the ambient dimension dependence in
64
and explicitly characterizes the interplay between approximation error and the manifold curvature.

4.1
Large and Small Noise Analysis

Theorem
4.4
builds upon separate analyses for large noise and small noise regimes. To construct the network class
\mathcal{F}
, we first construct sub-networks for the large noise and small noise regimes respectively, and then incorporate them by a time switching network. The network architecture is depicted in Figure
3
. We highlight the respective approximation guarantees in each regime.

Figure 3:
Illustration of the neural network architecture. A time switching network aggregates sub-networks for small noise and large noise respectively. Both regimes involve Taylor approximation of on-support scores, and projection approximation as part of orthogonal scores. These approximators are implemented by neural networks.

Large Noise

Inspired by Lemma
3.1
, we construct a network consisting of parallel structures for representing the decomposition in each chart. We summarize the result in the following lemma, whenever
t\geq t_{\rm large}
, a threshold for the large noise regime.

Lemma 4.5

(Large noise score approximation)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
hold. Given an approximation error
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a network class
\mathcal{F}_{\rm large}(L_{\rm large},W_{\rm large},S_{\rm large},B_{\rm large},\cdot)
that yields a function
\bar{s}_{\rm large}
, such that for any time
t\in[t_{\rm large},T]
with
t_{\rm large}=\log\frac{1}{1-\epsilon^{2/\beta}/4}
and
T=\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\epsilon))
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{s}_{\rm large}(x,t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(x)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{t})}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{h_{t}}\left(\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2/\beta}}{\tau^{2}}\right)^{\gamma_{0}}+\epsilon^{2(\beta+1)/\beta}\right)\right),

which holds for any
\gamma_{0}>0
.
The network configuration satisfies

\displaystyle\hskip 50.58878ptL_{\rm large}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1\right),\quad W_{\rm large}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(D^{\gamma_{0}}\epsilon^{-d/\beta}\right),

\displaystyle S_{\rm large}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(D^{\gamma_{0}}\epsilon^{-d/\beta}\right),\quad B_{\rm large}=\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon^{-2(\gamma_{0}+\log(1/\epsilon))/\beta}\right).

The proof is provided in Appendix
B.2
.
We observe that the network size scales with the intrinsic dimension
d
. Moreover, the approximation error depends on the curvature of the manifold. More specifically, a smaller reach implies more rapidly varying local geometry, which increases the approximation error through the curvature-dependent factor
(\epsilon^{2/\beta}/\tau^{2})^{\gamma_{0}}
. In addition, the network architecture comprises parallel sub-networks corresponding to individual charts, and a smaller reach necessitates finer local approximations that collectively enlarge the network. Compared to the approximate rate in Theorem
4.4
, we note that the large noise regime contributes a minor error term, while the dominating error owes to the small noise regime.

Small Noise

Similar to the large noise regime, we identify a threshold
t_{\rm small}
for the emergence of the small noise regime. The approximation guarantee is presented in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6

(Small noise score approximation)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
hold. For any sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, there exists a network class
\mathcal{F}_{\rm small}(L_{\rm small},W_{\rm small},S_{\rm small},B_{\rm small},\cdot)
that yields a function
\bar{s}_{\rm small}
,
such that for all time
t\in[t_{0},t_{\rm small}]
, with
t_{0}={\rm poly}(n^{-1})
and
t_{\rm small}
satisfying

\displaystyle h_{t_{\rm small}}=\min\left\{\epsilon^{2/\beta},\frac{\min\{\eta^{2}\tau^{2},1\}}{\max\{D\log(1/\epsilon),\sqrt{D}B\}}\right\},

we have

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{s}_{\rm small}(x,t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(x)\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{t})}^{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{D^{2\gamma+d+2}}{h_{t}}\left(\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2/\beta}}{\tau^{2}}\right)^{\gamma}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\right),

which holds for any
\gamma>0
. The configuration of
\mathcal{F}_{\rm small}
satisfies

\displaystyle L_{\rm small}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\gamma^{3}\right),\quad W_{\rm small}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(D^{\gamma}\gamma^{3}\epsilon^{-d/\beta}\right),\quad S_{\rm small}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\gamma D^{\gamma}\epsilon^{-d/\beta}\right),\quad B_{\rm small}=\mathcal{O}\left(h_{t_{0}}^{-\gamma}\epsilon^{-2}\right).

The proof is provided in Appendix
B.1
.
Lemma
4.6
guarantees that we can find a network class with size depending on the intrinsic dimension
d
to efficiently approximate the score function in the small noise regime.
Moreover, both the time threshold
t_{\rm small}
and the approximation error depend on the manifold curvature. Specifically, a small reach indicates rapidly changing local geometry, which substantially complicates the approximation of the interaction term
E_{2}
in Lemma
3.2
, leading to more stringent requirements on the time
t
and greater representation difficulty. When
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, the time threshold becomes
t_{\rm small}=\log\frac{1}{1-\epsilon^{2/\beta}}
, dominated by the approximation error.

Compared to the large noise regime, with a comparable number of nonzero parameters, neural network approximation suffers from an enlarged error. This is due to the finer approximation to the manifold in the small noise regime, instead of using coarse piecewise linear approximations based on tangent spaces. Consequently, when combining the large and small noise regimes, the small noise error is dominating.

The large and small noise regimes overlap in Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
with
t_{\rm large}<t_{\rm small}
, as shown in Figure
3
. This indicates that when
t\in(t_{\rm large},t_{\rm small})
, either of the score decompositions can be effectively approximated by neural networks. More importantly, the overlap introduces a “grace period” when switching from the large noise regime to the small noise one, where we linearly interpolate between the two regimes. This can be exactly realized by ReLU networks; see details in Section
4.2
.

4.2
Proof Sketch of Theorem
4.4

The complete proof of Theorem
4.4
is provided in Appendix
B
, which boils down to developing neural network approximation theories for the large and small noise regimes, respectively. For either the large or small noise regimes, the proof is constructive, which consists of two major components:
Step 1. Local polynomial approximation
to the on-support and orthogonal scores characterized in Section
3
, and
Step 2. Implementing local polynomials by neural networks
. After approximating the large and small noise scores separately, we complete the proof by
Step 3. Introducing a time-switching network
to actively select large and small noise score approximations.

We focus on the more challenging small noise regime to showcase
Steps 1
and
2
. The large noise regime replicates similar arguments. Recall from Lemma
3.2
that the on-support score
s_{\mathcal{M}}
can be written as

s_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)=\nabla\log\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{h_{t}}(E_{1}(t)+E_{2}(t))\right)\textrm{d}P_{\rm data}(x_{0}).

Expanding the gradient, we have

\displaystyle s_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)=\frac{s_{2}(x,t)/\sqrt{h_{t}}}{s_{1}(x,t)},

where

\displaystyle s_{1}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}}\exp\left(-\frac{E_{1}(t)+E_{2}(t)}{2h_{t}}\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\rm data}(x_{0})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle s_{2}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}}\frac{\alpha_{t}x_{0}-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)}{\sqrt{h_{t}}}\exp\left(-\frac{E_{1}(t)+E_{2}(t)}{2h_{t}}\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\rm data}(x_{0}).

It suffices to approximate
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
separately. Given their similarity, we demonstrate an approximation to
s_{1}
for presentation; the same arguments apply to
s_{2}
.

\bullet

Step 1. Local polynomial approximation.

We consider the atlas in Remark
2.8
on
\mathcal{M}
and adopt the decomposition of
P_{\rm data}
into local measures in Remark
2.14
to cast
s_{1}
into

\displaystyle\begin{split}s_{1}(x,t)=\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\int_{\mathcal{B}^{d}(0,r)}&\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}\underbrace{\|\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v)\|^{2}}_{E_{1}(t)}\bigg)\\
&\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}\underbrace{2\langle x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t),\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v)\rangle}_{E_{2}(t)}\bigg)F_{k}(v){\,\mathrm{d}}v,\end{split}

(4.3)

where
F_{k}(v)=\rho_{k}(\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v))\cdot p_{\rm data}(\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v))\cdot G_{k}(v)
is a local density-related function according to (
2.2
). Here we expand the expressions of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
for future reference. A critical advantage of (
4.3
) is the reduction of the original integration over
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
to local integration over balls in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, manifesting intrinsic dimension dependence.

We further simplify (
4.3
) by neglecting minor components for a more efficient approximation. We first show that the magnitude of interaction term
E_{2}
can be well-controlled by
E_{1}
and the reach of the manifold. In particular, by Lemma
D.6
, it holds that

\displaystyle|E_{2}(t)|\leq\frac{4\|x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)\|}{\alpha_{t}\tau}E_{1}(t).

Therefore, we restrict our attention to
E_{1}
being sufficiently small, i.e.,
E_{1}(t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(h_{t})
, so that
s_{1}
is not extremely small. Accordingly, within each chart, we consider a subset

\mathcal{C}_{k}=\big\{v\in\mathcal{B}^{d}(0,r):\|\alpha_{t}\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v)-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)\|\leq\Delta\big\}\quad\text{with}\quad\Delta=\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{\alpha_{t}h_{t}\log(1/\epsilon)}\big),

on which
E_{1}(t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(h_{t})
. A closer inspection on
\mathcal{C}_{k}
reveals that when the chart center
x_{k}
is distant from the given noisy state
x
,
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is empty. To reduce such a redundancy, we identify a selected index set

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(x)=\{k:\|x_{k}-x\|\leq L_{\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}}r+2\sqrt{Dh_{t}\log(1/\epsilon)}+\sqrt{D}Bh_{t}+\Delta\},

(4.4)

where by Lemma
D.3
,
\mathcal{C}_{k}
asserts an efficient
d
-dimensional representation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}=\mathcal{B}^{d}\big(v_{k}(x,t),L_{\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{x}}^{-1}\Delta\big),\quad\text{ where }v_{k}(x,t)=\mathrm{\mathbb{Log}}_{k}(\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)/\alpha_{t}).

(4.5)

To this end, we approximate
s_{1}
by

\begin{split}s_{1}(x,t)\approx\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(x)}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{k}}&\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}\underbrace{\|\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v)\|^{2}}_{E_{1}(t)}\bigg)\\
&\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2h_{t}}\underbrace{2\langle x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t),\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v)\rangle}_{E_{2}(t)}\bigg)F_{k}(v){\,\mathrm{d}}v.\end{split}

(4.6)

Compared to (
4.3
), we have effectively reduced the integration domain and the number of local charts in (
4.6
). The remaining arguments construct polynomials for representing the right-hand side of (
4.6
). While the detailed construction is rather technical, we highlight essential building components.

1.

Local density-related function
F_{k}
. We show that
F_{k}
is
\beta
-Hölder smooth, inheriting the regularity of the data distribution. Therefore, we use local Taylor polynomials of degree at most
\lfloor\beta\rfloor
for approximating
F_{k}
in Lemma
D.8
;

2.

Exponential function
\exp(\cdot)
. Both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are plugged into an exponential function, which is
C^{\infty}
. We use a Taylor polynomial of degree
\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\epsilon))
for approximating the exponential function of
E_{1}
. A different approach is adopted for
E_{2}
, where we consider a Taylor polynomial of degree
\gamma
. Note that
E_{2}
involves an inner product. When computing powers of
E_{2}
in the Taylor polynomial, it is convenient to use tensor inner products. Indeed, for any
j\leq\gamma
, we have
[E_{2}(t)]^{j}=\langle[x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)]^{\otimes j},[\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)-\alpha_{t}\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}(v)]^{\otimes j}\rangle
. Due to the linearity of the tensor inner product, we can extract common factors when summing over
k\in\mathcal{I}(x)
. Detailed construction of these polynomials are provided in Lemma
D.9
.

3.

Exponential map
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}
. The exponential map
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}
is also
C^{\infty}
on each chart. Different from the previous two components, we use average Taylor polynomials (
8
, see also Definition
F.1
) for approximation as detailed in Lemmas
D.11
and
D.13
. The average Taylor polynomial provides simultaneous accurate approximation to
\mathrm{\mathbb{Exp}}_{k}
and its first-order derivatives. This is critical to ensure that the approximation error of
E_{1}
being additionally proportional to
h_{t}
, leading to a tight approximation error to
s_{1}
when
t
is small.

Substituting the building components into (
4.6
), we obtain a polynomial approximation to the integrand, which depends on the noisy state
x
, the projection
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)
, and vector
v
. Integrating against
v
over the Euclidean ball
\mathcal{C}_{k}
yields another polynomial depending on
x
,
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)
, and center point
v_{k}(x,t)
, which approximates
s_{1}
. Note that the integrating tensors in
E_{2}
over
\mathcal{C}_{k}
leads to tensors with polynomial entries. We denote them as Tensor-Poly.
The following lemma presents the formal statement.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
hold. Consider any time
t\in[t_{0},t_{\rm small}]
with
t_{0}={\rm poly}(n^{-1})
and
t_{\rm small}=\log\frac{1}{1-\epsilon^{2/\beta}}
. Given a sufficiently small approximation error
\epsilon>0
and integer
\gamma>0
, consider a polynomial in
x
,
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)
and
v_{k}(x,t)
defined as

\displaystyle f(x,t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\gamma-1}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{j!h_{t}^{j/2}}\bigg\langle[x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)]^{\otimes j},\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(x)}\text{Tensor-Poly}^{k,j}(v_{k}(x,t),h_{t},\alpha_{t})\bigg\rangle,

where
\text{Tensor-Poly}^{k,j}
is an order-
j
tensor with each entry being a polynomial. Then for any
x\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},2\sqrt{Dh_{t}\log(1/\epsilon)}/\alpha_{t})
, it holds that

\displaystyle|f(x,t)-s_{1}(x,t)|=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(h_{t}^{d/2}\max_{j=0,1,\ldots,\gamma}\left\{\frac{\left\|x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)\right\|^{j}}{(h_{t})^{j/2}}\right\}\left(\frac{D^{(\gamma+d)/2}\epsilon^{\gamma/\beta}}{\tau^{\gamma}}+\epsilon\right)\right).

\bullet

Step 2. Implementing local polynomials by neural networks.

Given the polynomial approximation
f(x,t)
, we use neural networks to implement it, which is represented as follows,

\displaystyle\bar{s}_{1}(x,t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\gamma-1}\frac{1}{j!}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\times}^{j}\left({\mathfrak{N}}_{\rm proj}^{j}(x,h_{t},\alpha_{t}),\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\times}^{1}\left({\mathfrak{N}}_{\rm{det}}(x-x_{k}),{\mathfrak{N}}_{\rm poly}^{k,j}({\mathfrak{N}}_{v_{k}}(x,t),h_{t},\alpha_{t})\right)\right).

(4.7)

There are three key subnetworks: 1)
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\rm proj}^{j}
implements
[x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)]^{\otimes j}
, 2)
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\rm det}
is a chart determination network identifying index in
\mathcal{I}(x)
, and 3)
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\rm poly}
implements the Tensor-Poly. In addition,
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\times}
is a network for approximating the multiplication operation. In the sequel, we dive deeper into the construction of these three subnetworks, with full technical details deferred to Appendix
B.1
.

The three subnetworks take
h_{t}
,
\alpha_{t}
, and
v_{k}(x,t)
as inputs. Because
h_{t}=1-e^{-t}
and
\alpha_{t}=e^{-t/2}
are explicit univariate
C^{\infty}
functions, we treat them as known quantities in our analysis. Their neural network approximations would require only logarithmic network size relative to the approximation accuracy (see
(
52
, Lemma 3.3)
), contributing negligibly to the overall network complexity. The primary challenge lies in approximating
v_{k}(x,t)
defined in (
4.5
). The following
proposition proves that
v_{k}(x,t)
is
C^{\infty}
and thereby can also be efficiently approximated by a network
{\mathfrak{N}}_{v_{k}}(x,t)
as shown in Lemma
D.14
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Fixing any time
t>0
, the function
v_{k}(\cdot,t):\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
C^{\infty}
on
\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
for every
x\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
and every
k\in\mathcal{I}(x)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.4
, function
v_{k}(x,t)
is well-defined for
x\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
.
According to
41
,
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot,t)/\alpha_{t}
is a
C^{\infty}
function on
\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
, given that
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth manifold. By the definition of atlas in Definition
2.2
, the log map
\mathrm{\mathbb{Log}}_{k}
is
C^{\infty}
as well. Therefore,
v_{k}(\cdot,t)
is
C^{\infty}
as the composition of two
C^{\infty}
functions.
∎

Now we construct three subnetworks based on the elementary network
{\mathfrak{N}}_{v_{k}}(x,t)
.

1.

Projection network
{\mathfrak{N}}_{{\rm proj}}
. As shown in Proposition
4.8
, the projection
\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)
is a
C^{\infty}
-function of
x\in\alpha_{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{M},\tau)
. Thus each entry of
[x-\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)]^{\otimes j}
is
C^{\infty}
, which is approximated by
{\mathfrak{N}}_{{\rm proj}}^{j}
entrywise (Lemma
D.18
).

2.

Chart determination network
{\mathfrak{N}}_{{\rm det}}
. We identify relevant charts by evaluating the Euclidean distance
\|x-x_{k}\|
between the noisy state and the center of each chart. Therefore, we use a neural network to approximate an indicator function
\mathds{1}\{\|x-x_{k}\|^{2}\leq\bar{r}(t)^{2}\}
with
\bar{r}(t)
given in (
4.4
). This is plausible since the squared Euclidean distance can be well approximated by neural networks. See full details in Lemma
D.19
.

3.

Tensor-Poly network
{\mathfrak{N}}_{{\rm poly}}
. We implement each entry in Tensor-Poly
k,j
by a neural network. Aggregating these entries yields
{\mathfrak{N}}_{{\rm poly}}^{k,j}
(Lemma
D.20
).

Network
\bar{s}_{1}
in (
4.7
) approximates
s_{1}
. Similarly, we construct a network
\bar{s}_{2}
to approximate
s_{2}
. Then we have
\bar{s}_{\mathcal{M}}=\bar{s}_{2}(x,t)/(\sqrt{h_{t}}\bar{s}_{1}(x,t))
as the approximation to the on-support score, where the division is realized by an extra network (Lemma
B.3
. Combining
\bar{s}_{\mathcal{M}}
with an additive projection network yields the score estimator
\bar{s}_{\rm small}
in the small noise regime. We repeat
Steps 1
and
2
to construct
\bar{s}_{\rm large}
for the large noise regime in Appendix
B.2
.

\bullet

Step 3. Constructing a time-switching network.
To incorporate the large and small noise approximation
\bar{s}_{\rm small}
and
\bar{s}_{\rm large}
into a single score network, we implement a time-switching network consisting of two simple ReLU functions. For any
t\in[t_{0},T]
, we define two switching function as

\displaystyle{\rm SW}_{\rm small}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t_{\rm small}-t_{\rm large}}{\rm ReLU}\big((t_{\rm small}-t_{\rm large})-{\rm ReLU}(t-t_{\rm large})+{\rm ReLU}(t-t_{\rm small})\big),

\displaystyle{\rm SW}_{\rm large}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t_{\rm small}-t_{\rm large}}{\rm ReLU}\big({\rm ReLU}(t-t_{\rm large})-{\rm ReLU}(t-t_{\rm small})\big).

Recall that
t_{\rm small}
and
t_{\rm large}
are the thresholds for the large and small noise regimes. The shape of the switching functions is depicted in Figure
3
. Using the switching functions, we construct the score network as

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}(x,t)={\rm SW}_{\rm small}(t)\cdot\bar{s}_{\rm small}(x,t)+{\rm SW}_{\rm large}(t)\cdot\bar{s}_{\rm large}(x,t).

It can be seen that
{\rm SW}_{\rm small}(t)+{\rm SW}_{\rm large}(t)=1
for any
t\in[t_{0},T]
. Outside the overlapping interval
[t_{\rm large},t_{\rm small}]
, one of the switching functions is identically zero. Only within the overlapping interval, does
\widetilde{s}(x,t)
become a convex combination of the large and small noise score networks. It is straightforward to implement the multiplication operation in
\widetilde{s}
. This completes the construction of a neural network that approximates the ground-truth score function.

5
Sample Complexity of Score and Distribution Estimation

In this section, we provide sample complexity for score function estimation using the network class
\mathcal{F}
, and further establish distribution estimation guarantees using the estimated score function.

5.1
Score Estimation

Recall that we denote
\widehat{s}
as the empirical risk minimizer of denoising score matching in (
2.6
). By choosing the score network
\mathcal{F}
as in Theorem
4.4
, the following theorem establishes the generalization property of
\widehat{s}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

[Score estimation error bound]
Suppose Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
hold.
Let early-stopping time
t_{0}=n^{-c}
with some constant
c>0
and terminal time
T=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
.
We choose the network class
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}(L,W,S,B,R)
as in Theorem
4.4
with
\epsilon=n^{-\beta/(d+2\beta)}
, and
R=\mathcal{O}\left((n^{c}\log n)^{1/2}\right)
. For a sufficiently large
n>\tau^{-(d+2\beta)}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{T}-t_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left\|\widehat{s}(x,t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(x)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(p_{t})}{\,\mathrm{d}}t\right]\lesssim D^{2\gamma+d+2}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}},

where
\gamma=\lceil\beta(1+\log(\tau^{d+2\beta})/\log n)^{-1}\rceil
.

The proof is provided in Appendix
C.1
.
Theorem
5.1
demonstrates the efficiency of neural networks in learning the score function and their adaptivity to the low-dimensional data structure. We provide two remarks.

Dependence on Dimension

The estimation error of
\widehat{s}
converges at a rate
n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}
, depending exponentially only on the intrinsic dimension
d
and polynomially on the ambient dimension
D
. Familiar readers may find that this estimation error rate matches the mimimax rate of density estimation for
\beta
-Hölder densities
(
67
)
. An intriguing fact about score function estimation is that it inherits the regularity of the clean data distribution, as the score function itself does not directly verify regularity conditions.

Sample Size Depending on Curvature

The requirement on the sample size
n>\tau^{-(d+2\beta)}
is nontrivial when the reach is small, i.e.,
\tau<1
. In the case of
\tau\geq 1
, Theorem
5.1
holds for any sample size
n
. Consistent with previous discussions, a small
\tau
induces complicated local structures on the manifold, making it fundamentally challenging for learning. Therefore, the sample size needs to scale with the reach to ensure a plausible estimation.

5.2
Distribution Estimation

We transfer the score estimation guarantee to distribution estimation guarantee, since the backward process (
2.5
) is solely driven by the learned score function:

\displaystyle\textrm{d}\widehat{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Y}_{t}+\widehat{s}(\widehat{Y}_{t},T-t)\right]\textrm{d}t+\textrm{d}\overline{B}_{t},\quad\widehat{Y}_{0}\sim{\sf N}(0,I_{D}).

The backward process is terminated at an early-stopping time
T-t_{0}
. We denote the estimated distribution
\widehat{P}
as the distribution of
\widehat{Y}_{T-t_{0}}
. The following result bound the deviation of
\widehat{P}
to
P_{\rm data}
in Wasserstein distance.

Theorem 5.2

(Distribution Recovery Results)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
hold. Let
t_{0}=n^{-\frac{2(\beta+1)}{d+2\beta}}
,
T=\log n
and
\gamma=\lceil\beta(1+\log(\tau^{d+2\beta})/\log n)^{-1}\rceil
. Then for
n>\tau^{-(d+2\beta)}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{1}(\widehat{P},P_{\rm data})]\lesssim D^{\gamma+d/2+1}n^{-\frac{\beta+1}{d+2\beta}}.

The proof is given in Appendix
C.2
.
The distribution recovery rate matches the minimax optimal rate for learning a
\beta
-Hölder distribution supported on
[0,1]^{d}
, which is a special case of
d
-dimensional manifold.

Proposition 5.3

(Theorem 3 in
50
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\beta}_{d}
be a class of distributions satisfying Assumptions
4.1
and
4.3
with
\mathcal{M}=[0,1]^{d}
. Then for any
\beta>0
and
d\geq 2
, we have

\inf_{\widehat{P}_{n}}\sup_{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\beta}_{d}}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{1}\left(\widehat{P}_{n},P\right)\right]\gtrsim n^{-\frac{\beta+1}{d+2\beta}},

where the infimum is taken over all estimators
\widehat{P}_{n}
based on
n
observations.

Our theory shows that neural networks efficiently learn the score function, which in turn enables optimal distribution recovery. It validates that the generated samples will faithfully reflect both the geometric structure of the manifold and the distribution of the original data.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we have provided a theoretical framework for understanding diffusion models for low-dimensional data. By analyzing the score function’s structure and its dependence on the curvature, we have derived approximation and estimation guarantees. Our results demonstrate that diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality by adapting to the underlying structures of the data, with convergence rates governed by intrinsic dimension and manifold curvature. These insights not only explain why diffusion models excel in practice, but also highlight the importance of tailoring architectures to data structure.

Appendix A
Proofs in Section
3

We present the proofs of score decomposition for both large noise and small noise regimes.

A.1
Proof of Lemma
3.1

Lemma
3.1
decomposes the
score function via projections of the input
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
onto tangent spaces of
\mathcal{M}
.
The proof manipulates on the marginal density function
p_{t}
via the conditional transition kernel in the forward process. Conditioned on the initial clean data
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
, at time
t
, the marginal distribution of the noisy state is Gaussian
{\sf N}(\alpha_{t}x_{0},h_{t}I_{D})
. Therefore, the marginal density function
p_{t}
satisfies the following display,

\displaystyle p_{t}(x)=(2\pi h_{t})^{-D/2}\int_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\rm data}(x_{0}).

We consider the atlas on
\mathcal{M}
in Remark
2.8
. Then we use the associated partition of unity
\{\rho_{k}\}_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}
to rewrite
p_{t}
and
\nabla p_{t}
as follows:

\displaystyle p_{t}(x)=(2\pi h_{t})^{-D/2}\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\int_{x_{0}\in U_{k}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right)\rho_{k}(x_{0})p_{\rm data}(x_{0}){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0})

(A.1)

and

\displaystyle\nabla p_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=(2\pi h_{t})^{-D/2}\int_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}}-\frac{x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}}{h_{t}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right){\,\mathrm{d}}P_{\rm data}(x_{0})

\displaystyle=(2\pi h_{t})^{-D/2}\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\int_{x_{0}\in U_{k}}-\frac{x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}}{h_{t}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right)\rho_{k}(x_{0})p_{\rm data}(x_{0}){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0}).

Utilizing the projection
\Pi_{k}
onto the
k
-th tangent space, we have

\displaystyle\nabla p_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=(2\pi h_{t})^{-D/2}\sum_{k=1}^{C_{\mathcal{M}}}\int_{x_{0}\in U_{k}}-\frac{x-\Pi_{k}(x,t)}{h_{t}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-\alpha_{t}x_{0}\|^{2}}{2h_{t}}\right)\rho_{k}(x_{0})p_{\rm data}(x_{0}){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0})
